### Page 3

What could hurt the dollar?
A key risk to our constructive dollar view is a renewed rise in FX
hedge ratios among foreign investors. While conﬁdence in the
dollar has pushed hedge ratios back to very low levels, any
renewed White House intervention in ﬁnancial, trade or foreign
policy could prompt investors to increase hedging and weigh on
the dollar.
A big topic for FX strategists over the last 18 months has been
foreign investor FX hedge ratios on US assets. Data suggests these

have returned to very low levels again as conﬁdence has returned
to the dollar story. As we witnessed brieﬂy in August, when the US
Treasury extended the duration of its Treasury buy-back operations,
the dollar was hit by White House intervention in ﬁnancial markets.
Any fresh White House intervention in ﬁnancial, trade or foreign
policy, perhaps after the November midterms, could see the dollar
come under pressure as investors raise hedge ratios again.

On the subject of the midterms, the base case of the Democrats
winning the House looks unlikely to move FX markets much. But a
Democratic clean sweep of Congress, and what it means for a
potential government shutdown, regulation, and constraints on the
AI build-out, could prove a cyclical negative for the dollar.

Document 253: Germany _ An export-driven recovery (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Germany _ An export-driven recovery (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

10
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
AN EXPORT-DRIVEN RECOVERY
Growth, which began in the fourth quarter of 2025, gained momentum in the first half of 2026. This momentum should
continue, with expected growth of 1.1% in 2026 and 1.3% in 2027. In the short term, the main driver will still be exports,
particularly to Europe, which will contribute to restructuring industry. The impact of investment plans is expected to

strengthen in 2027, while domestic demand is likely to remain constrained by inflation (2.7% in 2026 and 2.6 % in 2027),
which will weigh on purchasing power. Fiscal stimulus and structural reforms are expected to continue to support growth.
However, the rise in the deficit and debt levels means that the increase in long-term interest rates observed so far is mostly
structural.
A RETURN TO GROWTH DRIVEN BY EXTERNAL TRADE
The first half of 2026 confirms the rebound in growth (0.4% and then

0.3% q/q in Q1 and Q2). This growth is driven by foreign trade (contribu-
tions from net exports of +0.8 pp and +0.2 pp, respectively, with +0.9 pp
on average coming from exports and -0.4 pp from imports), following
two years of contracting exports, particularly those to Europe (see be -
low). The increase in public expenditure ( see our analysis ) reflects
the implementation of investment plans, with the multiplier effect on
growth of these plans expected to increase.

In the second half of the year, growth is expected to remain robust,
pushing the annual average to 1.1% in 2026 (following 0.3% in 2025).
Exports are set to remain the main driver, thanks to continued strong
European demand, which is compensating for domestic demand that
remains sluggish. Growth of private consumption will, in fact, remain
subdued by energy inflation. Business investment is expected to be
driven by dynamic sectors (defence, technology equipment and aeros -

pace), which would limit the impact of rising interest rates.
In 2027, growth (1.3%) would be based more heavily on domestic de -
mand, with greater spillover effects from investment plans and a likely
recovery in household consumption (following a peak in inflation in
Q1 2027). At the same time, external demand is expected to remain
strong, but its contribution to growth would be lower than in 2026.
This is because the stronger growth in domestic demand would stimu-

late imports to a greater extent.
INDUSTRY IS BENEFITING FROM AI INVESTMENT AND INTRA-EUROPEAN
MOMENTUM
The business climate in industry improved significantly at the start of
the second half of the year (the manufacturing PMI has risen by 4 points
since May, reaching a four-year high in August). New export orders have
even reached their highest level since early 2022. These positive signs
put into perspective the risks associated with the summer’s climate

events (heatwaves and the historic drought on the Rhine), which dis -
rupted industrial activity and river transport.
Since early 2025, output in some sectors has rebounded, particularly in
the electronics sector (+6.4 %). Manufacturers of electrical equipment,
as well as IT and electronics firms, are benefiting in particular from
the wave of investment in data centres and defence. If this momentum
continues, it could offset some of the losses recorded in the automotive

and chemicals sectors, which have both seen the sharpest falls in out-
put since late 2017 (-12.8% and -19.4%, respectively).
The sustained increase in military spending on medical technologies
and robotics across Europe is driving a restructuring of the interme -
diate-goods sector. Companies exposed to struggling sectors are gra -
dually turning towards these new markets. However, this reorientation
is not fully offsetting the loss of historical markets.

The rebound in German industry is also being fuelled by European
demand. While exports to China (-12.7% YTD in July) and the United
States (-1.5%) have certainly continued to fall, exports to a number of
European Union countries have risen sharply, particularly in Southern
and Central Europe. This shift is benefiting machinery and transport
equipment (47.6% of goods exports, up 20.5% since January 2025) in
particular. We expect this momentum to continue in 2027, creating a

virtuous cycle that reinforces European growth.
A STAGNANT LABOUR MARKET
The situation in the German labour market has stabilised over the
past several quarters, but it is not yet showing signs of improvement.
The unemployment rate is shifting relatively little (4% in November;
+0.2 pp y/y) and remains below the Eurozone average (6.4%). Falling
employment in the manufacturing sector (-0.6% since Q4 2019) is offset
by an increase in the services sector (+0.9%). Nevertheless, the latest

business climate surveys suggest that the situation may stabilise. The
gradual retraining of the workforce in struggling sectors is helping to
meet some of the needs arising from new investment.
Generally speaking, in the longer term, pension reform should support
the labour supply. As a matter of fact, it will help to increase the num-
ber of workers gradually, notably through the phasing out of early reti-
rement at 63 and the indexing of the retirement age to life expectancy

from 2032 onwards.
GERMANY
Growth and inflation

The bank
for a changing
world
Although the unemployment rate is set to remain low, wage growth
would remain limited, particularly in 2026, as negotiated wages will
offset inflation to a lesser extent than they will in 2027. The planned
increases in the minimum wage (+8% in 2026 and +5% in 2027, to
EUR 14.6 per hour), significant as they are, will have little knock-on

effect on the rest of the wage scale.
PURCHASING POWER CONTINUES TO BE SQUEEZED BY HIGH INFLATION
Wage purchasing power is expected to fall further in 2026, having
already lost 4 pp since 2022, before stabilising in 2027. The reduction
in income tax (by EUR 10 billion, or 0.2% of GDP), which will come into
force next January, is also expected to support the disposable income of
low- and middle-income households.
Inflation has been rising again since the start of the summer and is

expected to remain above 2% throughout our forecast horizon. It has
accelerated since June (+0.5 pp to +2.9% y/yin August) driven by a fur -
ther rise in energy prices (+6.7 pp to +9.4% y/y), while core inflation re-
mains stable at +2.6%. From spring 2027 onwards, the expected easing
in energy prices (particularly gas prices) should bring down inflation.
However, this decline will be limited by the inflationary effects of the
wider roll-out of investment plans, as well as by the expected rise in

food prices.
This inflation, which has been structurally higher since the onset of the
2022 energy shock, will weigh on household consumption. Furthermore,
German households’ propensity to save remains high by international
standards (with a net balance 15 points higher on average over the
last ten years, according to the European Commission), which is also
weighing on their consumption.
FISCAL STIMULUS CONTINUES
After recording a public deficit of -3% of GDP in 2025, we anticipate a

further deterioration in the public balance to -4% in 2026, then -4.2%
in 2027. The primary deficit is projected to reach -2.8% of GDP in 2026
(after -1.9% in 2025) before narrowing slightly to -2.7% in 2027. At the
same time, the interest burden is expected to rise gradually from 1.1%
of GDP in 2025 to 1.5% in 2027. This combination will result in a rapid
rise in the debt-to-GDP ratio, from 62.6% of GDP in 2025 to 66.1% in
2026 and then 69.7% in 2027, before approaching 85% by the end of the

decade.
The yield on the 10-year Bund rose above 3.5% on 14 September, its
highest level since 2007. In addition to the increase in issues linked to
extra-budgetary funds, inflationary pressures are also contributing to
this rise. A gradual easing of inflation next year would make a mode -
rate easing of sovereign yields possible in 2027. However, yields would
remain high, at 3.2% in Q4 2027, suggesting that the bulk of the rise is
structural.
At the same time, the government is continuing to implement its pro -

gramme of structural reforms, including measures to support business
investment, health-insurance reform, pension reform and an overhaul
of the tax system. A number of measures have come into force, par -
ticularly accelerated depreciation for business investment until 2027,
which will be followed by a reduction in corporation tax from 2028.
Administrative simplification is also set to lead to an overhaul of the
tax system from January 2027 1. The health-insurance reform has been

passed, while the pension reform is still under discussion, with expecta-
tions that it will be adopted by the end of the year.
1  The main measure will be a reduction of EUR 10 billion per year in income tax for individuals on low incomes, funded in part by an increase in taxes on high earners. The bill will
be submitted to Parliament in the autumn, before being implemented from 2027.
Article completed on 14 September 2026
Lucie Barette
lucie.barette@bnpparibas.com

Document 257: GMO高管：股票超供和AI基建放缓，将共同引爆这轮AI泡沫

Source PDF: all report pdf/GMO高管：股票超供和AI基建放缓，将共同引爆这轮AI泡沫.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

GMO ⾼管 Inker 警告，史上最⼤规模 IPO 浪潮 ——SpaceX 、 OpenAI 、 Anthropic 合计将向
市场注⼊相当于美股市值 6% ⾄ 7.5% 的新增股票供给，这⼀规模远超互联⽹泡沫⾼峰期的历
史纪录。他指出，若叠加 AI 算⼒从紧缺转向过剩的拐点，市场跌幅可能达到 35% ⾄ 50% 。
摘要：
10 ⽉ 15 ⽇，美国通胀黏性推升加息预期，亚太半导体与存储产业链遭遇全线重挫， AI 算⼒独⻆
兽 CoreWeave 债券跌破⾯值 80% 、五年违约概率突破 50% 。
在这个全球科技资产剧烈动荡的关键节点，管理规模超 800 亿美元的波⼠顿知名资产管理机构
GMO ，由联席资产配置主管本 · 英克（ Ben Inker ）等联名近⽇发布了引发华尔街⾼度关注的三
季度重磅研报 —— 《 AI 泡沫破裂的催化剂》（ A Catalyst for the AI Bubble Break ）。该报告预
⾔：未来 12 ⾄ 18 个⽉内，前所未有的股权供给潮将刺破 AI 泡沫。

随后， Ben Inker 做客播客《 Other People's Money 》，阐述了他在三季度投资信中提出的判断。
他表⽰， SpaceX 、 Anthropic 、 OpenAI 三家公司的 IPO 及后续解禁，将在约 12 个⽉内向市场注
⼊相当于美股总市值 6% ⾄ 7.5% 的新增股票供给。
Inker 进⼀步指出，若 AI 算⼒同步从结构性紧缺滑向产能过剩， “ 我们将看到信贷端的⼤规模违
约，股权端的公司破产，以及超⼤规模云服务商投资回报率惨不忍睹的局⾯。那不是市场下跌
18% ，⽽是下跌 35% 到 50% 。 ”
历史上每⼀次技术⾰命都制造了泡沫
AI 是真实的技术变⾰，但真实的技术变⾰历来是泡沫的温床。 Inker 说：
在⼀般情况下，市场每增加 1% 的 IPO 市值占⽐，当年回报就会差 4 个百分点。如果我们看到 6%
的供给涌⼊，那就是⽐正常年份差 24 个百分点。
运河、铁路、汽⻋、电⽓化、互联⽹ —— 每⼀次变⾰性技术都制造了泡沫。每个⼈都能看出来，
天哪，这将改变世界。于是他们说，我真的想参与其中。

⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

问题在于，变⾰性技术的受益者极难事先判断，⽽⼤量涌⼊的资本本⾝就会摧毁回报。他以英
国铁路热潮为例：伦敦到曼彻斯特的第⼀条铁路利润丰厚，但到铁路热潮结束时，同⼀条线路
上建了六条铁路，没有⼀条能赚到钱。
落到 AI 的具体商业结构， Inker 认为问题同样清晰：⽆论是算⼒供给还是 AI 模型，都更像竞争性
⾏业，⽽⾮赢家通吃的格局。
他点出这⼀逻辑的商业终点： “ 如果 AI 要被⼴泛使⽤，它就必须便宜。⽽便宜的东西很难同时⼜
利润丰厚。”
史⽆前例的股票供给冲击正在逼近
Inker 的核⼼论点，是市场结构的变化使股票供给的冲击⼒远超历史。
美股过去数⼗年的底层⽜市逻辑，核⼼⽀撑之⼀在于上市公司持续的股票净回购。企业通过回
购不断回收流通筹码，年均供给缩减约 1% ，给⼤盘提供了稳定的被动买盘⽀撑。
眼下这套微观运⾏逻辑正在发⽣质变。 SpaceX 挂牌上市并开启⼤规模解锁周期， Anthropic 与
OpenAI 也加速⾛向公开市场。海量新筹码集中涌⼊市场，存量资⾦⾯临巨⼤的被动调仓压⼒。

投资者如果想要配置这些超级巨⽆霸，势必需要通过⼤量抛售现有持仓来腾挪资⾦，这会直接
压低⼤盘整体的估值中枢。
Inker 引⽤历史规律点出了这⼀⻛险的数学乘数：
这⼀轮潜在的供给洪流，在体量上已经刷新了历次科技狂热的纪录。 Inker 回顾了世纪之交的互
联⽹泡沫时期：
针对 SpaceX 在上市后采取的按批次递进解锁模式，英克认为这种精⼼设计的条款，恰恰反向证
实了发⾏⽅对⼆级市场承接极限的深刻忌惮：
当供给超过需求，不只是新增投资回报糟糕，它会把整个⾏业的存量投资价值⼀并摧毁。
这周 Anthropic 的模型最好，下周 OpenAI 的最好，还有来⾃其他实验室、其他国家的开源模型。
⼈们在切换，甚⾄在不同查询之间切换。现在有整个⾏业的存在，就是帮你在任务级别优化这种
切换。
“ 从历史规律来看，新 IPO 占市场总市值的⽐例每上升 1% ，当年美股的表现就会⽐正常年份落后
⼤约 4% 。如果后续进⼊⼆级市场的新增供给达到市值的 6% ，市场回报率将⽐常态低出整整
24% 。 ”
“在 1999 年和 2000 年，进⼊市场的新增供给占总市值的 2.5% 到 4% 左右。此前历史上还从来没有

哪⼀年出现过超过 4% 的供给增量。如果 OpenAI 和 Anthropic 如期推进 IPO ，加上 SpaceX 后续
的流通盘释放，全美新增股权供给可能达到总市值的 6% ⾄ 7.5% ，这是过去 12 个⽉维度上从未⻅
过的规模。”

### Page 3

算⼒过剩：从 “ 短缺溢价 ” 到 “ 旧资产清零 ”
股票供给冲击是 Inker 判断的第⼀层，算⼒从紧缺转向过剩则是可能将市场推向极端情景的第⼆
层。
他描述了⼀个⾃我强化的过剩逻辑：⽬前⼤量算⼒消耗在 AI 模型训练上。⼀旦市场意识到训练投
⼊过度，训练需求下降，原本被训练占⽤的算⼒就会释放出来，整个市场的算⼒供给将骤然宽
松。
在基础设施端，数据中⼼天然追求标准接⼝与硬件互通，业内甚⾄开始尝试将算⼒打包成标准
化的期货合约。⼀旦算⼒蜕变为⼤宗商品，科技巨头便⽆法单靠出租算⼒来获得持续的超⾼资
本回报。
眼下 CoreWeave 债券跌破 80 美分、亚洲存储芯⽚⻰头集体⼤跌，正是资本市场开始对 AI 算⼒现
⾦流产⽣信任危机的直接表现。
“SpaceX 管理层很清楚冲击有多⼤。即便采取分期解锁，也改变不了总量的现实。在接下来的 12
个⽉⾥，⼤约价值 2 万亿美元的 SpaceX 股票将陆续具备出售条件。持有者必然会卖掉很⼤⼀部
分，⽽场内买家只能靠抛售⼿头的其他股票来接盘。这对整个⼆级市场的流动性构成了沉重考

验。 ”
“ 现在的算⼒⽀出带有极强的内耗性质。⼤约⼀半的算⼒完全耗费在训练新模型上。如果⾏业突
然达成共识，发现⼤家在模型训练上烧了太多冤枉钱，把训练算⼒占⽐从 50% ⼀路削减到 10% ，
市场上瞬间就会出现海量被闲置出来的可⽤算⼒。 ”
如果我们都缺算⼒，那我当然愿意为⼀块五年前的 GPU 付钱，哪怕它能效更低。但如果世界突
然算⼒泛滥，那块五年前的数据中⼼还不如直接关掉 —— 因为我⽤最新的数据中⼼⽣成⼀个
token 消耗的电更少，⽽且机房容量完全管够，客⼾没有理由再为空余的⽼旧硬件付费。

### Page 4

Inker 指出，供求失衡绝不会仅仅停留在产业层⾯，它会⽴刻沿着资⾦链条引爆⾦融市场的系统
性震荡：
他特别区分了不同公司的脆弱程度。微软的数据中⼼投资回报率可能令⼈失望，但微软不会违
约。 “Coreweave 不会那么幸运。 Super Micro 可能也不会。 ” 他说：
他还指出，泡沫破裂往往先于基本⾯恶化被证实。
信贷市场：过度集中的隐患已在积聚
除股票市场外， Inker 对信贷市场同样表达了担忧，尽管他认为⽬前尚未出现系统性危机的明确
信号。
他指出，当前⾼收益债和投资级债券的利差均处于历史低位，约为历史第 13 百分位。与此同
时， AI 相关债务正在信贷市场中形成⾼度集中的敞⼝。
他还注意到，评级较低的三 C 级债券利差已经⾛阔⾄历史第 74 百分位，与⾼评级垃圾债的分化明
显。 GMO 信贷团队正在⼩幅介⼊，但尚未形成⼤规模配置，原因在于许多⾼⻛险债务结构建⽴
于 2020 ⾄ 2021 年零利率环境下，在当前 5% 利率⽔平下已⽆法正常运转，需要重组。
对于政府能否充当最后的救助者， Inker 持保留态度。

“ 如果这种局⾯发⽣，信贷市场将⾯临⼤⾯积违约，纯 AI 概念的上市企业会直接⾛向破产，超⼤
规模云⼚商的投资回报率也将变得⾮常糟糕。这种情况下的市场跌幅绝对不会只有 18% ，⽽是可
能演变为 35% 到 50% 的深度崩盘。 ”
这不是说⼀定会发⽣，但如果发⽣了，我们真的不应该感到惊讶 —— 因为这是每⼀次真正⾰命性
技术变⾰都曾经历过的模式。铁路改变了世界，汽⻋改变了世界，互联⽹改变了世界，纳斯达克
跌了⾄少 80% 。
在互联⽹泡沫中，泡沫破裂时，互联⽹流量并没有真的每三个⽉翻四倍这件事还没有被证实。是
其他因素先把它拖下来的。这⾥也很容易发⽣同样的事情。
如果所有计划中的投资都如期落地，这将成为⼀个深刻的问题 —— 企业债中 AI 相关敞⼝的集中
度，将超过现代信贷市场历史上的任何时期。

### Page 5

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

写评论
不过他认为，美联储仍有降息空间，且 AI 相关损失⽬前主要集中在股权端⽽⾮银⾏体系，这与互
联⽹泡沫破裂时的情况类似 ——“ 那次损失了很多钱，但没有动摇太多东西，因为损失不在⾼杠
杆机构⼿⾥，不在银⾏⼿⾥。只要损失在正确的地⽅，就可以有重⼤损失⽽不发⽣灾难。 ”
⾮ AI 资产：寻找 “ 有 AI 加成但不是 AI 押注 ” 的标的
⾯对上述⻛险， Inker 的配置逻辑是寻找那些本⾝具备合理估值和回报，同时能从 AI 需求中获得
额外收益的公司，⽽⾮将整个投资组合押注于 AI 本⾝。
他以⽇本公司 Toto 为例： GMO 的⽇本股票团队持有 Toto ，最初看中的是其卫浴业务，后来发现
Toto 凭借对陶瓷材料的深度积累，还有⼀块⽤于半导体传输的陶瓷部件业务，意外成为 AI 供应链

受益者。
在更宏观的配置层⾯， Inker 表⽰ GMO 偏好⾮美股票⽽⾮美股，偏好价值股⽽⾮成⻓股，并⻓期
超配资源类股票和⾼质量股票。他的理由是：资源股与其他资产的相关性随时间拉⻓⽽持续下
降，在通胀冲击情景下具有独特的对冲价值；⾼质量股票则在承担较低经济⻛险的同时，并不
需要以更低的⻓期回报作为代价。
对于韩国、中国台湾等 AI 受益市场，他的态度相对温和。
我不认为政府还负担得起充当救助者的⻆⾊。我们有⼤量债务，⾚字规模很⼤，我不知道钱从哪
⾥来，⽽且如果你动摇了国债市场，你造成的损害可能⽐你能提供的帮助更⼤。
我们寻找的是如何构建⼀个不只是 AI 押注、但有 AI 加成的投资组合。
这些公司利润涨了 10 倍，股价涨了 7 倍。我们仍然持有，因为利润增速快于股价涨幅，但我们减
少了⼀些，因为我们不认为这些利润完全可持续。

Khuyến nghị: MUA – Giá mục tiêu 
25,950 VNĐ/CP  
 Chúng tôi duy trì dự phóng tăng trưởng tín dụng 2026 đạt 27%, với động lực chính 
đến từ phân khúc KHDN, đặc biệt là từ nhóm FDI trong bối cảnh dòng vốn FDI vào 
Việt Nam và hoạt động xuất nhập khẩu duy trì tích cực. Xét về lĩnh vực, cho vay kinh 
doanh BĐS kỳ vọng sẽ tăng trưởng vượt trội khi ngân hàng tham gia vào thu xếp 
vốn cho các dự án hạ tầng và dự án BĐS ưu tiên, trong khi các mảng kinh doanh 
khác tăng trưởng ở mức khả quan.

Chúng tôi duy trì dự phóng NIM giảm so với năm 2025 do áp lực huy động khiến chi 
phí vốn duy trì ở mặt bằng cao, trong khi lãi suất cho vay dự kiến ở mức cạnh tranh 
để ưu tiên mục tiêu tăng trưởng tín dụng.

Chúng tôi dự phóng tỷ lệ nợ xấu của ngân hàng sẽ tăng 11bps YoY trong bối cảnh lãi 
suất đang neo ở mức cao và kỳ vọng ngân hàng sẽ tiếp tục duy trì chính sách trích 
lập dự phòng thận trọng nhằm củng cố bộ đệm, qua đó hoàn thành mục tiêu duy 
trì tỷ lệ bao phủ nợ xấu ở mức trên 100%.

Dựa trên triển vọng kinh doanh của ngân hàng cũng như đánh giá các yếu tố rủi ro, 
chúng tôi tiếp tục duy trì khuyến nghị MUA với cổ phiếu MBB với giá mục tiêu 
25,950 VNĐ/CP - tiềm năng tăng giá 26.2% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 17/09/2026.

Cơ cấu tín dụng  Hoạt động kinh doanh

Ngân hàng TMCP Quân đội (MBB) được 
thành lập năm 1994 với định hướng hoạt 
động ban đầu là cung cấp các dịch vụ tài 
chính cho các doanh nghiệp quân đội. Sau 
hơn 30 năm phát triển, MBB hiện đã trở 
thành ngân hàng sở hữu quy mô tài sản và 
tín dụng lớn nhất trong nhóm NH TMCP tư 
nhân. Phát triển theo mô hình tập đoàn, 
MBB đi đầu trong lĩnh vực chuyển đổi số với 
số lượng khách hàng và tỷ lệ CASA đầu 
ngành. 
Nguồn: MBB, KBSV

mức vượt trội so với ngành.

HOẠT ĐỘNG KINH DOANH 
Kết quả kinh doanh tích cực nhờ 
tăng trưởng thu nhập lãi thuần 
 Tín dụng trong 2Q2026 tăng mạnh so với quý trước, đạt 13.2% YTD cùng sự 
phục hồi của NIM đã giúp ngân hàng thiết lập kỷ lục mới về thu nhập lãi thuần 
với 16,894 tỷ đồng (+36.5% YoY). Thu nhập ngoài lãi tiếp tục thu hẹp trong quý 
này do hoạt động kinh doanh ngoại hối và chứng khoán kém tích cực, qua đó 
phần nào làm giảm tốc tăng trưởng tổng thu nhập hoạt động (+18.5% YoY). Chi 
phí dự phòng rủi ro đạt 4,247 tỷ đồng (-11.3% YoY), qua đó giúp LNTT tăng tích 
cực (+40.7% YoY), đạt 10,560 tỷ đồng. Lũy kế 1H2026, LNTT của ngân hàng đạt 
20,188 tỷ đồng, hoàn thành 51% kế hoạch BLĐ đề ra đầu năm.

Bảng 1. Cập nhật Kết quả kinh doanh 
(tỷ VNĐ) Q2/2025 Q1/2026 Q2/2026 +/-%YoY +/-QoQ Chú thích

hồi của NIM trong quý 2 đã hỗ trợ thu nhập lãi thuần tiếp tục 
mở rộng.  
Thu nhập ngoài lãi  4,874   2,517   3,541  -27.3% 40.7% Thu nhập ngoài lãi tiếp tục ghi nhận tăng trưởng âm trong quý 
này khi thu từ các hoạt động kinh doanh chứng khoán và 
ngoại hối kém tích cực (lần lượt giảm -51.2% và -74.3% YoY). 
Thu từ phí cũng cho thấy sự giảm nhẹ (-5.7% YoY) chủ yếu đến 
từ hoạt động tư vấn và xử lý, định giá & quản lý tài sản chưa 
khả quan, trong khi hoạt động thanh toán và môi giới vẫn 
tăng tích cực.

tăng chậm hơn giúp cho tỷ lệ CIR thu hẹp so với cùng kỳ.

Tăng trưởng tín dụng (YTD) 12.3% 3.3% 13.2%   Tăng trưởng tín dụng hợp nhất trong quý 2 đã tăng nhanh hơn 
đáng kể so với quý 1, đạt 13.2% YTD, tuy nhiên vẫn thấp hơn 
so với VPB và HDB - hai ngân hàng tư nhân cũng nhận CGBB. 
Cơ cấu tín dụng của ngân hàng tính tới hết quý 2 nghiêng về 
phân khúc KHDN (+17.2% YTD, chiếm 59.7% tổng tín dụng). 
Xét về lĩnh vực cho vay, động lực tăng trưởng tiếp tục đến từ 
kinh doanh BĐS (+38.2% YTD) và cho vay hộ gia đình (+15.4% 
YTD). 
Tăng trưởng huy động (YTD) 15.0% -0.3% 7.1%   Tăng trưởng huy động đã có sự cải thiện so với quý 1, nhưng 
vẫn thấp hơn đáng kể so với tăng trưởng tín dụng, khiến 
chênh lệch giữa hai chỉ tiêu tiếp tục nới rộng. Cơ cấu huy động 
tiếp tục được củng cố bởi kênh giấy tờ có giá (+24.7% YTD) và 
liên ngân hàng (+15.2% YTD), trong khi kênh huy động truyền 
thống vẫn chưa khả quan (+4.6% YTD).

với cùng kỳ nhờ các khoản vay đã được tái định giá theo mặt 
bằng lãi suất mới và ngân hàng gia tăng cho vay trung dài hạn. 
NPL 1.60% 1.42% 1.45% -15bps +2bps Chất lượng tài sản vẫn được duy trì trong quý này khi tỷ trọng 
của các nhóm nợ không ghi nhận sự biến động mạnh.

### Page 5

5

Tăng trưởng tín dụng 2026 được dự 
báo khả quan so với cùng kỳ  
 Chúng tôi duy trì dự báo tăng trưởng tín dụng cả năm 2026 ở mức 27%, vượt 
trội hơn mặt bằng chung của ngành nhờ lợi thế tham gia nhận CGBB. Cụ thể: 
− Xét về phân khúc khách hàng, động lực chính kỳ vọng sẽ đến từ phân 
khúc KHDN, trong đó nổi bật là nhóm FDI khi ngân hàng đã chủ động 
thành lập khối Khách hàng FDI chuyên biệt trong bối cảnh dòng vốn 
FDI vào Việt Nam và hoạt động xuất nhập khẩu duy trì tích cực.  
− Xét về lĩnh vực, chúng tôi cho rằng cho vay kinh doanh BĐS sẽ tăng 
trưởng vượt trội với kỳ vọng ngân hàng tham gia vào thu xếp các dự án 
hạ tầng và dự án BĐS ưu tiên (BĐS KCN, NOXH). Ngân hàng cho biết tỷ 
trọng dư nợ mảng này dự kiến sẽ được duy trì trong biên độ mục tiêu 
13±2% tổng dư nợ tín dụng. Chúng tôi nghiêng về khả năng ngân hàng 
sẽ bám sát cận trên của mục tiêu (15%), nhằm tận dụng dư địa tăng 
trưởng từ các dự án ưu tiên, đồng thời duy trì kiểm soát chất lượng tài 
sản an toàn. Trong khi đó, dư nợ cho vay các mảng khác dự kiến sẽ ở 
mức thấp hơn tăng trưởng chung do môi trường vĩ mô còn nhiều khó 
khăn.

Biểu đồ 8. Cơ cấu tín dụng 
Tỷ trọng cho vay kinh doanh BĐS liên tục gia tăng 
trong các quý gần đây, trong khi cơ cấu cho vay 
thương mại và chế biến chế tạo có xu hướng giảm. 
Tính tới hết 1H2026, tăng trưởng cho vay kinh doanh 
BĐS, thương mại, và chế biến chế tạo lần lượt đạt 
38.2%, 4.9%, và 9.3% YTD.

Nguồn: MBB, KBSV

NIM chịu áp lực giảm so với 2025  Chúng tôi duy trì dự phóng NIM cả năm sẽ giảm so với 2025 (-15bps YoY) dựa 
trên luận điểm:  
(1) Ngân hàng sẽ tiếp tục dịch chuyển cơ cấu huy động sang các kênh có 
chi phí vốn (COF) cao hơn nhằm đáp ứng mục tiêu tăng trưởng tín 
dụng cao, trong bối cảnh cạnh tranh huy động trên thị trường 1 vẫn 
diễn ra gay gắt. Bên cạnh đó, ngân hàng cho biết dự kiến sẽ phát hành 
10-15 nghìn tỷ trái phiếu tier 2 trong 2H2026 với COF tương đối cao. 
(2) Với mục tiêu tăng trưởng tín dụng cao, chúng tôi cho rằng ngân hàng 
sẽ duy trì lãi suất cho vay ở mức cạnh tranh so với mặt bằng chung của 
ngành. Các khoản vay dự kiến vẫn sẽ tiếp tục tái định giá theo mặt 
bằng lãi suất hiện tại, tuy nhiên tốc độ sẽ chậm lại so với quý 2, do đó 
lợi suất IEA dự kiến sẽ tăng chậm hơn COF trong 2H2026.

Chất lượng tài sản suy giảm trong 
tầm kiểm soát  
 Trong 2Q2026, tỷ lệ hình thành nợ xấu ở mức 1.2% (-16bps QoQ), tương ứng 
4,142 tỷ đồng nợ xấu phát sinh mới, và tỷ lệ nợ xấu tăng nhẹ 3bps so với quý 
trước. Nợ xấu tăng trong quý này chủ yếu tới từ phân khúc bán lẻ (do hoạt động 
kinh doanh và giá đất của khách hàng tại địa phương bị ảnh hưởng sau khi sáp

Cho cả năm 2026, chúng tôi dự phóng tỷ lệ nợ xấu của ngân hàng sẽ tăng 11bps 
YoY trong bối cảnh lãi suất đang neo ở mức cao. Cùng với đó, chúng tôi kỳ vọng 
ngân hàng sẽ tiếp tục duy trì chính sách trích lập dự phòng thận trọng nhằm 
củng cố bộ đệm, qua đó hoàn thành mục tiêu duy trì tỷ lệ bao phủ nợ xấu ở mức 
trên 100%.

Định giá – Khuyến nghị: MUA – Giá 
mục tiêu 25,950 VNĐ/CP  
 Chúng tôi kết hợp 2 phương pháp định giá là P/B và Chiết khấu lợi nhuận thặng 
dư để tìm ra mức giá hợp lý cho cổ phiếu MBB. 
(1) Phương pháp định giá P/B 
Chúng tôi điều chỉnh P/B mục tiêu xuống mức 1.5x trong bối cảnh lãi 
suất neo cao và thanh khoản ngân hàng còn nhiều khó khăn. Dù vậy, 
P/B mục tiêu vẫn cao hơn so với P/B trung bình 5 năm của ngân 
hàng nhằm phản ánh kỳ vọng tích cực đối với việc room ngoại của 
ngân hàng được nới lên 49% theo Nghị định 69/2025/NĐ-CP. 
(2) Phương pháp chiết khấu lợi nhuận thặng dư  
Bên cạnh đó, chúng tôi kết hợp sử dụng thêm phương pháp chiết 
khấu lợi nhuận thặng dư để phản ánh rủi ro hệ thống và kỳ vọng dài 
hạn. 
Khuyến nghị MUA  với cổ phiếu MBB với giá mục tiêu 25,950 VNĐ/CP - tiềm 
năng tăng giá 26.2% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 17/09/2026.

Document 292: Nuclear energy_ One industry, many reactors Our latest primer explores the reactor technologies and hurdles shaping nuclear’s next chapter. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Nuclear energy_ One industry, many reactors Our latest primer explores the reactor technologies and hurdles shaping nuclear’s next chapter. 2 min read.pdf
Report source: Bank of America
Date: 2026-09-01
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

13022014     1

Sustainability 
Nuclear energy: One industry, many reactors 
29 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• As power demand grows, nuclear has returned to the forefront of the energy conversation. Yet the industry is far from one-size-
fits-all, encompassing a range of reactor technologies designed to serve different markets, applications and deployment models. 
• Nuclear power is evolving beyond traditional large-scale reactors. While proven designs continue to anchor utility-scale 
generation, small modular reactors (SMRs) and microreactors are being developed to serve a broader range of applications 
through more flexible, modular deployment models, though most remain in the demonstration phase today. 
• The future of nuclear deployment will depend on more than reactor design alone. Supply chains, skilled labor, fuel availability and 
regulatory approvals remain critical challenges, though recent policy initiatives are intended to support licensing, fuel production 
and broader industry growth.

Nuclear energy has re-emerged at the center of the power conversation, driven by rising electricity demand and tightening 
supply-demand dynamics across the grid. Yet nuclear is not a single technology. It encompasses a wide range of reactor designs 
and sizes, each with distinct economics, risks and deployment pathways. Building off our previous nuclear publication (which can 
be found here: Nuclear energy: Who, what, where, when, why?), this primer unpacks that complexity, grounding the broader 
nuclear narrative in the practical realities of development, from technology and construction to fuel, licensing and execution.  
Reactor sizes 
Nuclear power is best understood as a collection of reactor classes differentiated by scale, deployment model and end use 
(Exhibit 1). Rather than competing directly with one another, these technologies are designed to serve different segments of the 
power market and, in many cases, can scale alongside one another. 
Exhibit 1: Different reactor types have different advantages and risks 
Comparison of reactor technologies

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
I NSTI TUTE
Accessible version

### Page 2

2 29 September 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Key Distinctions  
• Large reactors (≥1 gigawatt (GW)): Designed for large-scale electricity generation, these reactors provide consistent, high-
capacity power and serve as core assets within the grid. 
• Small modular reactors (SMRs)(~50–300 megawatts (MW)): Mid-scale systems designed for modular deployment across 
utilities, industrial users and large commercial customers, offering greater flexibility in siting and capacity expansion.  
• Microreactors (≤20 MW): Small, highly distributed systems intended for niche or off-grid applications where reliability, 
autonomy or energy security are primary considerations. 
Technology 
Nuclear power generation is based on a common principle: heat produced from nuclear fission is used to generate steam, which 
drives a turbine to create electricity (Exhibit 2).  
Exhibit 2: In a nuclear reactor, heat is formed by fission 
Process for a nuclear fission reaction

Source: BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Nuclear reactor designs can be grouped based on coolant type, neutron spectrum and fuel configuration. Most reactors currently 
operating in the US are light-water reactors (LWRs), though a wide range of advanced designs are being developed (Exhibit 3).  
Exhibit 3: Gen III & Gen IV+ technologies have different characteristics 
Reactor technologies 
Reactor Type Coolant Moderator Neutron Spectrum Operating Temperature Pressure 
Pressurized Water Reactor (PWR) Water Light water Thermal ~300–330°C High (~150 bar) 
Boiling Water Reactor (BWR) Water Light water Thermal ~280–290°C High (lower than PWR) 
Heavy Water Reactor (HWR) Heavy water Heavy water Thermal ~300°C High 
Molten Salt Reactor (MSR) Molten salt Graphite (typical) Thermal / fast (design-dependent) ~600–750°C Low 
High-Temperature Gas Reactor (HTGR) Helium gas Graphite Thermal ~700–800°C Low 
Sodium Fast Reactor (SFR) Liquid sodium None Fast ~450–550°C Low 
Gas-Cooled Fast Reactor (GFR) Helium gas None Fast ~700–850°C Low 
Source: BofA Global Research, Energy Information Administration (EIA), Idaho National Laboratory (INL), Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
AP1000 
The AP1000 is a Generation III+ pressurized water reactor with a capacity of approximately 1.1 GW per unit (Exhibit 4). Its design 
is centered around passive safety systems that rely on gravity, natural circulation and compressed gas, rather than large numbers 
of active pumps and diesel-backed safety systems. By reducing the number of safety-critical moving parts, this approach lowers 
system complexity and supports a more standardized large-reactor platform.

### Page 3

29 September 2026    3

I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 4: AP1000s typically need about 1,000 acres of land 
Diagram of an AP1000 nuclear plant

Source: BofA Global Research, Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
The AP1000’s design emphasizes open-top construction, with large structural and equipment modules manufactured offsite and 
then assembled at the project site. This approach is intended to reduce onsite labor needs, improve quality control through 
factory fabrication and support a more repeatable, standardized construction process. 
The AP1000 is no longer a conceptual technology. It is a licensed, utility-scale nuclear design already operating in the field. As a 
result, the key remaining considerations center on construction costs, project execution and the economics of deploying 
additional units, according to BofA Global Research. 
SMRs 
Small modular reactors (SMRs) are nuclear power plants generally 300 MWe or less per unit and are designed around a factory-
based, modular construction model (Exhibit 5). Unlike traditional gigawatt-scale nuclear projects, SMRs represent a shift toward 
repeatable, standardized deployment (read our prior SMR publication: Q&A quick guide: Small modular reactors). Major 
components can be manufactured in a factory setting and then transported to project sites for assembly, reducing the amount of 
onsite construction required. Their standardized designs also create opportunities for learning-by-doing, potentially lowering 
costs over time. In addition, capacity can be added incrementally through discrete modules, allowing capital to be deployed in 
stages rather than committed upfront.

### Page 4

4 29 September 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

Exhibit 5: SMRs typically require 50-200 acres of land, depending on design and location 
Diagram of an SMR

Source: BofA Global Research, Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Market landscape  
There are dozens of SMR designs globally, with more than 100 projects at various stages of development, though only a limited 
number are currently operational or under construction. While developers are pursuing a range of reactor technologies, light-
water reactor (LWR) designs still dominate the US development landscape (Exhibit 6). According to BofA Global Research, the 
sector remains largely in a development and demonstration phase rather than broad commercial deployment. 
Exhibit 6: LWRs make up 50% of the SMR designs 
US SMR designs by technology type (%)

Source: Energy Information Administration (EIA), BofA Global Research 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

I NSTI TUTE

Microreactors 
Microreactors are nuclear power systems typically 20 MWe or less per unit, representing the smallest class of advanced reactors 
(Exhibit 7). They emphasize flexibility, allowing deployment where traditional grid-scale infrastructure is impractical. Many 
designs incorporate multi-year fuel cycles that reduce refueling requirements and associated logistics burdens, making them 
particularly attractive for remote communities, industrial sites and defense-related applications. 
Exhibit 7: Microreactors need about one to five acres of land depending on the design 
Diagram of a microreactor

Source: BofA Global Research, Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Market landscape  
A growing number of developers are pursuing microreactor technologies in the US, however, despite increasing interest, most 
projects remain in the demonstration and pilot phase rather than commercial deployment. According to BofA Global Research, 
federal government programs continue to play a central role in funding and advancing early deployments, with several initial 
projects targeted for later this decade. 
Supply chain 
Large reactors depend on specialized components and complex construction processes, limiting the number of qualified 
suppliers. Modular designs, on the other hand, shift a greater share of manufacturing from construction sites to factory 
environments, with the goal of improving consistency, reducing onsite complexity and enabling more standardized deployment. 
As reactor sizes decrease, component requirements generally become less specialized, expanding the potential supplier base and 
simplifying project execution. 
Workforce availability and quality assurance remain critical across all reactor types. Large projects require significant numbers of 
skilled tradespeople, including welders, electricians and nuclear-certified inspectors. The US nuclear workforce has declined over 
prior decades, leading to labor shortages during first-of-a-kind builds.  
Fuel 
Fuel is one of the most important building blocks in the advanced nuclear ecosystem, as availability, manufacturing capacity and 
regulatory approval are all critical to deployment. Because many next-generation reactors require specialized fuels, the

### Page 6

6 29 September 2026 
  I NSTI TUTE

development of commercial-scale fuel supply chains will be a key factor in determining how quickly advanced nuclear 
technologies can be brought to market. 
HALEU 
High-assay low-enriched uranium (HALEU) refers to uranium enriched above the roughly 5% U-235 content used in conventional 
reactors but below the 20% threshold for highly-enriched uranium. HALEU enables smaller reactor cores and longer fuel cycles, 
making it a key requirement for many advanced reactor designs. 
Historically, commercial HALEU supply has been limited. To address this gap, the Department of Energy launched the HALEU 
Availability Program in 2024 to support enrichment, fuel processing and deconversion capacity. The government is also working 
to recover HALEU from existing materials, with a targeted recovery of roughly 10 to 26 metric tons over time. 
The first US HALEU production in decades occurred in 2023, though output remains limited. Current production is approximately 
900 kilograms annually and is government-owned, with material allocated primarily to research, fuel qualification and early 
demonstration projects. 
TRISO 
Tri-structural isotropic particle fuel (TRISO) is key for high-temperature gas-cooled reactors (HTGRs) and certain microreactors. 
Unlike conventional nuclear fuel, it requires specialized manufacturing and therefore is currently only limited to pilot-scale 
production. But key development efforts include DOE-led research through the Advanced Gas Reactor (AGR) program and 
expansion of private-sector manufacturing with DOE support. 
Regulatory approval is also required for new fuel designs, manufacturing processes and transportation systems. As a result, 
scaling TRISO production remains an important step toward the broader deployment of advanced reactors. 
Fuel Risk 
Fuel availability is a key execution risk for advanced nuclear projects. Many next-generation reactor designs rely on HALEU or 
TRISO fuel, both of which remain supply-constrained. As a result, reactor technology may be ready before fuel supply is, creating 
a potential bottleneck for deployment.  
Challenges include multi-year lead times for fuel production and certification, potential gaps between reactor licensing and fuel 
readiness, and the limited supplier base and transportation requirements associated with HALEU. Together, these factors can 
add uncertainty to project timelines and costs until fuel supply chains reach commercial scale. 
Licensing 
Licensing is a key determinant of nuclear project timelines, capital deployment and execution risk. Regulatory requirements 
shape not only whether a project is approved, but also how predictable construction schedules are and how easily reactor 
designs can be replicated. In the US, commercial reactors are licensed by the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC), whose 
framework emphasizes safety reviews, environmental assessments and public participation. While this approach provides 
regulatory certainty, it can also create lengthy approval timelines. 
Large light-water reactors benefit from well-established regulatory processes, while frameworks for advanced reactors continue 
to evolve. For first-of-a-kind projects, licensing can extend four to eight years or longer depending on technology maturity, 
application quality, public intervention and regulatory resources.  
Policy  
Recent years have seen a flurry of initiatives to revitalize nuclear energy. A series of coordinated policy moves, demonstration 
programs, fuel supply efforts and industry partnerships have converged to create the conditions for a potential nuclear 
resurgence:  
• ADVANCE Act: Signed into law in July 2024, the ADVANCE (Accelerating Deployment of Versatile, Advanced Nuclear for 
Clean Energy) Act modernizes the NRC licensing process (making it more affordable and predictable). The legislation also  
facilitates the siting of new reactors at brownfield sites, supports domestic fuel-cycle capabilities, and includes broader NRC 
institutional reforms, such as workforce recruitment and retention initiatives, updates to the agency’s mission statement and 
provisions related to international nuclear cooperation and exports. 
• Executive orders: On May 23, 2025, the Trump administration issued four executive orders aimed at accelerating US nuclear 
deployment through licensing reform, expedited reactor testing, expanded national security applications and efforts to 
strengthen the domestic nuclear fuel and supply chain base. Since their issuance, federal agencies, particularly the 
Department of Energy, have taken steps to implement these directives. While progress has been made on several fronts, a 
number of the orders' longer-term objectives remain under active implementation, according to BofA Global Public Policy.

Report Introduction
Our proprietary dbDataInsights survey shows cyber threats are evolving faster than organisations can adapt
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.
“It’s the only thing that can shut down my business.”
That was the punchline from a conversation I had recently with the CEO of a large
privately held financial services company. This was his comment when I followed up

as to why cybersecurity risk is the number one concern that “keeps him up at night”.
To help understand this risk, our dbDataInsights team surveyed more than 50
cybersecurity experts. The uncomfortable conclusion is that many firms are
unprepared for cyber threats. Cyber risk is increasing as AI adoption accelerates.
—89% agree the threat landscape is evolving faster than organisations can adapt.
—78% say the gap between highly and poorly prepared organisations is widening.

What has changed with AI advancement? Cyberattacks have become faster,
cheaper, more deceptive and more scalable. Capabilities once reserved for
sophisticated actors are now broadly accessible to a new generation of profit-driven
criminals. At the same time, rising geopolitical tension has made cyber operations
an increasingly attractive low-cost, deniable tool for nation states. Corporates and
investors therefore need to understand their broader exposure and critical

infrastructure risk in their supply chain and with stakeholders.
Among other autonomous incidents, the recent Hugging Face incident
highlighted AI’s cyberattack potential. AI agents caused harm without
direction from a human attacker, with weak guardrails and no human
judgement to restrain them. Beyond the systemic questions it raised, the attack
highlights three additional concerns: (i) AI operates at a speed and scale humans
cannot match, with agents taking over 17,000+ actions in four days; (ii) the intrusion

began in the data-processing pipeline, where AI platforms are uniquely exposed;
and (iii) the agents escaped their test environment through a widely used third-party
tool representing supplier risk in an unfamiliar form.

### Page 4

Report summary and notable takeaways
Secular trends and AI adoption have widened the attack surface; the severity of losses is compounding
Source: FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3), company security advisories, OECD, company reports, Deutsche Bank Research.
Secular trends have widened the attack surface. This includes rapid
digitalisation, widespread Internet of Things (IoT) endpoints, hybrid workforces,
cloud adoption and deeper supply chain dependencies. The scale of the threat

increase is reflected in data from the FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3),
which shows cybercrime complaints more than doubled from 2019 pre-pandemic
baseline of ~467,000 to ~1.0mn in 2025, with a 3x expansion relative to 2017 levels.
Barriers to entry are lower; attack tools are now sold “off the shelf”. Today,
cybercrime marketplaces, available on the dark web, look less like the underworld
portrayed in films and more like an online software store. Buyers can access AI-

powered tools that scan for software vulnerabilities (see top-right chart showing
reported CVEs after the release of cyber capable frontier models), write malicious
code at speed, and provide access to malware that rewrites its own code to avoid
detection as it runs. Ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) takes it further, renting out
ready-made attack kits, often in exchange for a portion of the profits.
Impersonation is becoming harder to detect. Hackers can now generate more

realistic phishing messages, while voice cloning and deepfakes make impersonation
convincing enough to gain access unnoticed. With stolen credentials, attackers often
aren’t hacking in, they are simply logging in.
The rise of loose cybercriminal collectives. Some of the most damaging recent
attacks have been linked to loosely organised, often English-speaking networks such
as ShinyHunters and Scattered Spider. The latter has been widely named as the

group behind the 2025 Marks & Spencer cyberattack, in which the hacking group
deployed RaaS. AI is enabling these groups to become larger and faster, widening
the gap between the speed of attack and a defender’s response.
The severity of losses is compounding. A 2026 IBM study puts the average breach
at a record USD ~5.0mn, up 12% YoY, but the impact on large companies can run
into hundreds of millions or even billions. Estimates for global cyber insurance

premiums (per Munich Re) point to a rise from roughly USD 7.0bn in 2020 to USD
15.0bn in 2025, with expectations to reach USD 28.0bn by 2030.

### Page 5

5
Among the more concerning survey takeaways is that a large-scale and
significant systemic incident is now the base case.
— 83% expect a significant systemic attack within two years and 81% expect it to be
moderately, very, or extremely severe for economic activity and markets.
— 81% say AI favours attackers today, with not one respondent putting defenders
ahead of attackers. Only 20% expect defenders to be ahead in five years.
Geopolitical tensions coupled with rising cyber capabilities have transformed

cyberspace into a digital battlefield between global powers. Intent has moved
towards espionage and disruption of not only critical infrastructure (e.g., healthcare,
finance, power, industrial systems, defence) but private companies as well. Chinese
President Xi Jinping has linked cybersecurity to national security and economic and
social stability, with China’s public statements similarly emphasising the protection
of critical infrastructure. At the same time, the US’s June 2025 Executive Order on

Cybersecurity reflects its continued focus on national cyber resilience.
Europe relies on US cyber tools while its start-ups lag in funding. Reliance on US
security vendors exposes European firms to potential access, update or export
restrictions over the long-term. Shrinking this gap will prove difficult given US cyber
start-ups attract nearly 6x the venture and growth funding of European peers. And
while the timing of quantum computing (which could break widely used encryption

methods) remains uncertain, it could further deepen Europe’s dependency.
Ultimately, cyber preparedness is a financial variable. Investors should ask what
firms spend on security versus AI adoption, how well they know their vulnerabilities,
and how fast they can recover.
— Only 24% of respondents think organisations have sufficient visibility into the
threats relevant to them, even though 57% saw cybersecurity budgets rise over
the past 12 months.

— 81% agree that cybersecurity related laws and regulation cannot keep pace, and
65% doubt leadership understands cyber risk well enough.

### Page 6

6
When cyber weakness = share price consequence…
Investors are more aware of cyber risk today, but they rarely sell a stock due to
perceived cyber vulnerabilities (although we are aware of some who have).
—What is the potential impact? Our dbDataInsights event study of 47 breaches
shows a cumulative breach-attributed return of c. -2.9% within the first two
trading days and c. -6.0% through first 20 days trading days. The erosion then
continues more gradually, reaching c. -6.7% nine months after disclosure (see

slide 13).
Which industries are most vulnerable?
—Our survey respondents point to (1) government & public sector (52%), (2)
financial services (50%), (3) energy and utilities (46%) and (4) healthcare and life
sciences follow (39%, rising to 48% among US respondents).
We should caveat, that high sector exposure does not mean every company in it is
equally vulnerable. Some firms in these “exposed” sectors may very well be better
prepared given they have built specialist teams and defence protocols for decades.

What about access to credit and ratings? Credit and rating providers are paying
attention. Moody’s and S&P both cut their outlook on Jaguar Land Rover within two
months of its 2025 hack. Today, this type of impact is not widespread. But if risk
escalates through a systemic incident or an AI-driven step change in attacks these
mechanisms, including ESG ratings, could reprice asset ownership or impact access
to credit. In fact, some lenders are already pricing it. Journal of Corporate Finance

research using US syndicated loan data found borrowers with persistently higher cyber
risk pay 4–13bp more.
The bottom line? While a broader systemic attack is hard to prepare for, corporates
and investors should be asking questions about vulnerabilities, security investment,
preparedness and recovery should an attack occur. This is especially true for
industries quickly adopting AI capabilities and / or a high threat landscape.

dbDataInsights Cybersecurity Expert Survey methodology
—This survey was conducted among 54 cybersecurity professionals across the US
and Europe in September of 2026. Questions took the form of either multiple

choice selections or free form responses. The dbDataInsights team received the
responses anonymously, but respondents were required to work or consult in
cyber security roles
—Who took the survey? On self-identified roles, around 80% sit inside an
organisation’s cybersecurity function, with the remainder working independently
(15%) or in academia and research (6%). The most common titles were security
architect (23%), Chief Information Security Officer (16%) and security analyst (9%),

alongside Head of Security, Deputy CISO, Incident Response Lead and Security
Operations Lead.
—Only a small minority of respondents work for cyber resellers, thus survey views
are primarily attributable to those working within an organization on
cybersecurity.
—Survey participants were also asked for their perspectives on cyber resilience and
governance, what is misunderstood, alongside emerging threats, risk to AI
adoption and the influence of geopolitics.

—While responses were anonymous, in some cases we conducted follow up
interviews with certain participants where contact details were made available.
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research.

### Page 10

Risk from AI-enabled attacks and added AI adoption vulnerabilities
Insider threats and supply chain compromises remain a growing concern
“The fear of AI writing undetectable malware is … overblown. Attackers were
never short of malware, since they can buy it ready made along with initial
access. Good detection looks at behaviour rather than exact code, so a rewritten
payload still has to do the same suspicious things.”
“Frontier AI models are currently the main hype. Automated phishing and

scams are underappreciated, yet they are responsible for the bigger
monetary losses at the moment.”
“There are too many organisations relying on the same providers for
critical services and this means single points of failure.”
“A corporation might be very secure but if they rely on a third party that
does not have the same level of security posture, [it] could be
compromised via this third-party (basically their weak link).”
“Existing employee login behaviour and usage of AI is underappreciated –

it’s what poses the biggest threat to a breach.”
“No company can guarantee they won’t be breached, so there needs to
be more focus put on the ability to recover and survive an attack.”
Source: dbDataInsights survey of cybersecurity experts, September 2026; Cybersecurity Survey 2026, Deutsche Bank Research .

### Page 11

11
Experts believe governments are not keeping pace, and no one owns AI oversight
How experts surveyed by dbDataInsights view governance and regulation
—AI adoption is outpacing internal controls. As one expert put it: “Companies
are pushing the use of AI more to their employees without the proper
mechanisms and policies in place to prevent misuse or attacks initiated by the AI
systems themselves.”
—Regulation is struggling to keep pace. 81% of respondents agree that

cybersecurity-related laws and regulations are failing to keep up with
technological change, and 54% say regulatory requirements can improve security
but may also slow innovation. One respondent highlighted “the levels of
uncertainty, the long running nature of the problems, and the rapidity of change.”
—Responsibility for AI oversight remains unsettled. International bodies are the
leading choice, supported by 50% of respondents. No option commands a

majority, underscoring the lack of consensus on who should oversee AI
development.
—Better board questions may not guarantee better decisions. The survey
points to more informed questioning by corporate leadership, yet 65% doubt that
leadership teams understand cyber risk well enough to make informed business
trade-offs. Measuring activity more effectively than outcomes further complicates
oversight.
—Accountability is becoming personal. Only 43% say accountability for cyber risk

is clearly defined in their governance framework, yet the consequences now reach
the top as a handful of CEOs have left or gone into early retirement after
financially consequential cyber breaches.
Who should primarily oversee AI development?
dbDataInsights survey – September 2026, based on overseeing development while managing cyber risk

Material cyber breaches can cause a notable drop in share price
Our dbDataInsights study found a 2.9% drop on average within two trading days, with the cumulative breach-attributed return of -6.0% on trading day 20.

Source: dbDataInsights, Bloomberg Finance LP., Deutsche Bank Research, Company filings. Event study of reported cybersecurity incidents, 2015 -2025, 34 US and 13 European companies across nine sectors.
What typically happens to a company’s shares when a breach is reported?
—Short-term impact: The market prices the breach immediately. Our model shows
cumulative breach-attributed (i.e. isolating only the part of the share price move
we can pin on the breach itself) returns are c. -2.9% within two trading days of

at one year, although the one-year point estimate (our single best-guess figure)
should be treated with caution given the width of the confidence band.
—Pre-breach price behaviour: We find no evidence of information leakage ahead
of disclosure. While the breach component for the [-10,-1] window (ten trading
days before the news broke) is +0.04%, the corresponding t-stat value of 0.03 falls
notably below the usual bar for significance (c.2). As such, we conclude the market

had not begun reacting before the announcement.
Event study methodology
—Sample: 47 publicly reported cybersecurity incidents across the US and Europe
over 2015-2025, measured against each company’s regional sector benchmark.
Requirement of 5 years of stock returns pre-attack and 1 year of post-attack.
—Day 0: the day an incident was first publicly reported or disclosed.
—Baseline: We use five years of pre-incident share-price performance to
estimate: (i) Beta (β): how sensitive a company’s returns are to movements in its

sector and (ii) Alpha (α): the extent to which the company typically outperforms
or underperforms its sector after adjusting for that sensitivity.
—Attribution: post-incident returns are split into sector-relative returns, alpha
(α) and breach (abnormal returns attributed to the disclosure).
—95% confidence interval: Range within which we can say with 95% confidence
that the real share price impact lies, based on our sample data.
Total return attribution

Marks & Spencer (Reported Apr 2025)
What happened?
A breach via an attack on a connected 3rd
party forced the suspension of all online
orders and led to empty store shelves.
What did it cost?
~£136mn of direct costs related to the
attack, estimated group operating profit
impact of around £300mn for the FY. M&S
also said they had received £100mn via
cyber insurance.
Coupang (Reported Nov 2025)
What happened?
A data breach exposed the records of
more than 30mn users with a former

employee reportedly behind the attack.
What did it cost?
The firm was fined more than ~625bn won
($409mn) (2/3rd directly attributable to
data leak), and also announced a $1.2bn
voucher compensation scheme.
UnitedHealth (Reported Feb 2024)
What happened?
Hackers reportedly used stolen credentials
to access a subsidiary’s (Change
Healthcare) systems, stealing data and
deploying ransomware.
What did it cost?
Total costs exceeded $3bn, with the CEO
summoned to testify before Congress,

where he confirmed that a $22mn ransom
was paid.
Equifax (Reported Sep 2017)
What happened?
A data breach saw hackers expose
sensitive customer information, including
145.5mn Social Security numbers and
more than 200k credit card numbers.
Attackers reportedly moved inside Equifax
systems for 76 days before being detected.
What did it cost?
Settlements, fines and legal costs
exceeded $1bn, while the firm also spent
more than $1.5bn on tech upgrades

following the incident.

Notable cyber attacks: how they happened, estimated costs and share price reaction, cont.
Nike (Reported Jan 2026)
What happened?
A cybercrime group leaked what it claimed
was 1.4TB of internal Nike data (appearing
to cover metrics such as product
specifications and pricing), following expiry
of a ransom deadline.
What did it cost?
While Nike did confirm it was investigating
the incident, it did not report any
operational disruption. Underperformance

shown in the chart does not appear to be
related to the breach.
T-Mobile (Reported Aug 2021)
What happened?
An attacker reportedly targeted an
exposed router as an entry point, before
using brute force attacks to gain further
access to T-Mobile systems.
What did it cost?
In 2022 the firm agreed to pay $500mn to
settle a class-action lawsuit ($350mn
payment to class members  plus $150mn
on security spend) but has since suffered
further data breaches, contributing to an

additional $31.5mn regulatory settlement.
Coinbase (Reported May 2025)
What happened?
Attackers bribed at least one support
agent at a connected 3rd party to access
Coinbase support systems and steal
customer data, then tricked users into
sending crypto.
What did it cost?
Coinbase refused to pay the $20mn
ransom demanded by the attackers to
keep the attack quiet. Instead they
reimbursed affected users and cited costs
of up to $400mn. The stock impact was

short-lived, though MSCI applied an ESG
score deduction.
Jaguar Land Rover (Reported Sep 2025)
What happened?
Production was shut down for five weeks
due to what was deemed a ransomware
attack; though no ransom was asked for
(later reporting suggested potential Russia
links were being investigated).
What did it cost?
JLR posted £485mn Q2’ FY26 loss (vs.
£398mn profit in Q2’ FY25), with an
additional £196mn in “cyber-related costs”.
These impacts filtered through to the

share price of JLR’s Parent (Tata Motors),
while UK economic impacts were
estimated at £1.9bn.

### Page 16

16
Targets go beyond tech and can also impact critical infrastructure
MGM Resorts (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
A vishing call to the IT help desk was reportedly used to reset credentials, after which
ransomware took down hotel systems, bookings and casino floor operations for several
days.
What did it cost?
MGM declined to pay a ransom. Around $100mn hit to third-quarter 2023 results plus
roughly ~$10mn of additional one-time expenses related to the incident.

Caesars Entertainment (Reported Sep 2023)
What happened?
The same social-engineering group as in the MGM incident targeted an outsourced IT
support vendor, gaining access to the loyalty programme database and member data.
What did it cost?
Caesars paid roughly $15mn of a reported $30mn demand and kept customer-facing
operations running.
NHS / Synnovis (Reported Jun 2024)
What happened?
Ransomware against pathology provider Synnovis significantly reduced its capacity to

process tests leading to delays / cancellations of over 11,000 appointments.
What did it cost?
Estimated 2024 direct operational financial impact of around GBP 32mn for Synnovis,
several times the profit reported in the prior year, plus wider NHS disruption costs.
Colonial Pipeline (Reported May 2021)
What happened?
A compromised VPN credential without multi-factor authentication allowed attackers to
access the network where they subsequently deployed ransomware. The operator shut the

pipeline down, triggering fuel shortages (c. 70% of gas stations in some states ran out of
fuel) across the US East Coast.
What did it cost?
A $4.4mn ransom was paid, of which about $2.3mn was later recovered by the US
authorities.
Bitget (Reported Sep 2026)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a vulnerability in a 3rd party security product to steal internal network
credentials, forge withdrawal instructions, bypass risk controls and ultimately steal crypto.

What did it cost?
An estimated $388mn worth of funds was transferred out by the attackers. While the firm
reimbursed affected users via its User Protection Fund, there were significant outflows
following the incident as users moved to take funds off the platform.
Progress Software (Reported May 2023)
What happened?
Attackers exploited a zero-day SQL injection vulnerability in Progress Software’s MOVEit file
transfer system. This was then used by attackers to steal data from firms running the

Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026

### Page 17

Cyber breaches can impact consumer behaviour
Roughly 4 in 10 consumers we surveyed say they would stop buying from a company they shop with after a known breach or attack.
Source: dbDataInsights monthly consumer survey, Deutsche Bank Research. EU Average includes France, Germany, Italy and Spain.
How will customers respond to a data breach once it occurs?
dbDataInsights survey of consumer responses, % of respondents
How will customers react once the breach is resolved?

Consumer responses among those who would either stop buying or were unsure, % of respondents
Consumer surveys suggest that cyber breaches can undermine customer
loyalty.  Results of our dbDataInsights Consumer survey show the potential
mindset of a consumer after a cyber breach occurs:
—Across the US, UK and EU average, 39–41% say they would stop buying, while only
27–28% would continue. Around a third are unsure.
—Among those who would stop buying or are unsure, only 18–22% say they would

return as normal. Another 30–35% would buy selected products only, 17–20%
would not return, and 28–29% remain unsure. Note that this implies a range of
13-15% believe they would not shop at the store again after the breach is
resolved.
Admittedly, these stated intentions may overstate actual switching, particularly for
essential purchases where alternatives are limited. Nevertheless, the findings
suggest that cyber breaches that put customer privacy at risk can influence

purchasing decisions, especially if they have alternatives.

dbDataInsights Consumer Survey Methodology
—Our proprietary survey conducted in 2026 (January – September) asked

consumers how they would respond if a company they shopped at regularly
was involved in a data breach or cyberattack, and how they would respond
once the disruption was resolved. This monthly survey covers households and
consumers in Germany, France, Italy, Spain, the United Kingdom, and the
United States.
—For each country referenced above, a nationally representative sample of more
than 500 households is surveyed each month (over 600 households in the US).

Collectively, this meant over the course of the survey (January – September
2026), we surveyed almost 30,000 households.

### Page 18

18
Material cyber incidents have contributed to negative credit outlooks
The infrastructure exists for cyberattacks to have a greater impact on credit quality should cyber disruption continue to accelerate
Source: S&P, Moody’s, Fitch, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Credit rating agencies consider both pre-breach resilience frameworks and
post-breach financial impacts. Agencies assess pre-breach cyber preparedness
through a lens of mitigating risk, while post-breach credit impacts centre on

financial capacity to withstand the resulting shock (considers scale and length of
the disruption); highly leveraged issuers with limited liquidity are seen as more
vulnerable. Negative outlook changes at JLR and Equifax illustrate the potential
credit impact. Fitch generally considers permanent reductions in cash generation
more credit-significant than temporary cash-flow delays.
—Ratings agencies are moving towards continuous monitoring. In 2021,

Moody’s invested $250mn in BitSight, which produces daily security ratings.
Moody’s has since built BitSight’s ratings into its credit tools, including Credit
Catalyst and EDF-X, for uses such as underwriting and ongoing monitoring of
borrowers and suppliers. This means lenders can see a company’s exposed
vulnerabilities as a potential indicator of corporate cyber risk.
S&P Global:
“The increasing frequency of cyberattacks and the potential for rapid deterioration in credit

profiles after an attack are risk factors relevant for our rating assessments.”
Moody’s:
“Cyberattacks can undermine the creditworthiness of companies, organizations and
governments, across all sectors and geographies, hitting revenues, increasing costs, and eroding
customer trust.”
Fitch Ratings:
“Although cyber events cannot be predicted with great accuracy, the associated risk can be
managed through proactive strategies, technology, policies, and vigilance.”

“The chief executive officer and board of directors hold ultimate responsibility for managing
cyber risk.”
Example rating actions where cyber was a named factor

Cyber governance & breaches can impact ESG scores
Sectors that hold the most personal data, and monetise it most directly, carry the greatest risk weighting in mainstream ESG scores
Source: MSCI ESG Ratings Methodology, Morningstar Sustainalytics ESG Risk Ratings, SASB Materiality Map, Deutsche Bank Resear ch.
Cybersecurity is generally seen as both a social and governance consideration
MSCI and Morningstar Sustainalytics both assess data privacy and security through management of the

risk, exposure to it and the controversies that follow a breach. Weak governance weighs on the score,
and a breach can cut it directly – more severe controversies draw larger deductions that persist for
longer.
Materiality varies sharply by sector. SASB flags data security as material across most consumer,
financial, healthcare and technology industries, with customer privacy concentrated in the most data-
intensive ones (see right).

### Page 20

IV. Investment, risk and opportunity

### Page 21

21
Europe’s dependency on US tech is firmly in the spotlight
Access to frontier AI models is now a security question; the funding gap for Europe will be hard to close
Source: Tikehau Capital – Tikehau Capital Barometer of European Investment in Cybersecurity – 7th edition 2026, Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank Research.
—Growing cyber risk creates increased dependency on US cyber tech. Growing
concern over the cyber capabilities of frontier AI models has made ensuring

access to such technology a national security priority. US dominance over the AI
tech chain (e.g. chips, model development, etc), its willingness to impose AI-
related controls / restrictions, and previous pressure against Europe over tech
regulations has amplified concerns that dependence on US tech represents a
strategic threat to European sovereignty.
—Funding is the structural challenge. US cybersecurity start-ups attract almost 6x
the funding of European peers, and US-domiciled firms dominate the major

indices – 89% of the Nasdaq CTA Cybersecurity index and 93% of Global X
Cybersecurity ETF (which seeks to track the performance of Indxx Cybersecurity
Index) by weight. Europe has also seen home-grown firms such as Darktrace
acquired by US companies, though this reflects broader market trends rather
than anything cyber-specific. Ultimately, the US has a greater exit strategy for
start-ups as well.
—Boosting homegrown solutions a focus, but also a risk. EU policymakers are

seeking to address the dependency; e.g. the EU’s Cybersecurity and AI Action Plan
includes “scaling up Europe’s AI capabilities for cybersecurity” as one of its three
objectives. However, there are also risks associated with this endeavour; as one
dbDataInsights survey participant put it, “for most European organisations, the
bigger near-term danger would be moving too fast to less capable providers.”
—The wider relationship adds to the exposure. A more transactional

international environment, alongside Europe’s dependency on the US for energy
supplies and defence support, compounds the risk. Recent US-EU diesel
negotiations serve as a pertinent example of this, whereby the US reportedly
considered a diesel export ban to compel a release of European diesel reserves.

### Page 22

22
Cybersecurity as an opportunity
Tailwinds include demand related to keeping operations secure with AI acceleration and consolidation tailwinds
Source: dbDataInsights, Deutsche Bank Research. Investors, defined as those who invest in stocks/ shares (either individually or through a broker/advisor) on a regular basis , were asked whether they perceived cybersecurity / data privacy as a risk or an opportunity for investors in 2026.
Broadly, DB’s company research view is that cybersecurity becomes even

more important in the AI era. Whereas cybersecurity has historically been viewed
as a blocker/drag on innovation and operational speed, it is now viewed as a
strategic enabler for secure AI adoption.
In particular, network security may see renewed growth after being viewed as a
more mature category. Rising AI usage should drive additional compute and data-
centre capacity, expanding the infrastructure requiring segmentation and
protection. Agentic workloads may also generate lower-throughput but more

frequent and persistent interactions across models, tools and data sources,
increasing the volume and complexity of network-level inspection.
Vendor consolidation has been a structural trend in cyber for some time, though not
necessarily linear. That said, the current environment may accelerate the
consolidation tailwinds as customers look to close security gaps that may arise from
a fragmented tool stack.
See further commentary from DB’s company research team here.

Our March 2026 “personal investor” survey showed a more positive view
among “ESG” investors. 58% in the US and 52% in the UK saw cybersecurity/data
privacy as an opportunity. Among all investors, US respondents favoured
opportunity over risk (46% vs 30%). UK views were more balanced (38% vs 40%).

### Page 24

24
Appendix A - Attack types
Top Attack types What it is How AI is helping attackers
Malware Software built to damage, disrupt or gain unauthorised access AI writes harmful software faster. Google has found malware that rewrites its own code while running to avoid detection, and criminal marketplaces selling AI malware-building tools.
Phishing Deceptive messages that trick people into giving up sensitive information Where AI’s effect is clearest: fluent, personalised lures without the old spelling and phrasing giveaways. Google says state-backed groups use AI to mass-produce them, and AI can fake voices and video.

Denial of service (DoS) Flooding a system so it cannot serve legitimate users Little reported evidence so far. AI can help find weak points and automate traffic floods, but the attack itself stays fairly simple.
Distributed denial of service
(DDoS)
The same attack launched from many
compromised devices at once
AI can accelerate the development and automation of attack tooling, while large-scale
botnets provide the distributed capacity behind hyper-volumetric DDoS attacks.

Man-in-the-middle (MitM) Intercepting or altering traffic between two parties Mainly indirect. AI-written phishing pages and deepfake voices make it easier to trick people into connecting through an attacker or approving fake sign-ins.
SQL injection Malicious SQL queries injected to manipulate backend database operations AI tools scan sites and spot flaws in database code faster, so less-skilled attackers can find weaknesses that once needed real expertise.

Cross-site scripting (XSS) Scripts injected into web pages to hijack user sessions As with SQL injection: AI speeds up finding vulnerable pages and writing the malicious code.
Zero-day exploits Attacking a software flaw before a patch is released A major new development. In May 2026 Google reported the first zero-day it believes was developed with AI. Its September 2026 report says the bigger effect is attackers misusing known flaws faster, before fixes land.

Advanced persistent threats
(APTs)
An intruder staying hidden in a network for long-
term
Google reports state-backed groups using AI across the whole attack, from target
research and lures to tooling and sifting stolen data.
Ransomware Encrypting data or locking device and demanding payment to release it NCSC expects AI to add to the global ransomware threat by lowering the skill needed to break in. AI also helps criminals find the most extortable data.

IoT exploitation Abusing weakly secured connected devices for entry AI can scan the internet for unpatched cameras, routers and storage devices and identify which flaws they carry.
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, National Cyber Security Centre, Google Threat Intelligence Group, Cloudflare, FBI Internet Crime Complaint Center (IC3)

Appendix B - European vs US cyber regulation
Europe US Federal
Debbie Jones | dbDataInsights | Under Attack | October 2026
Regulation Key requirements
NIS2 Directive In force 2023, transposition deadline Oct-24. Covers 18
sectors (up from seven) and creates an EU vulnerability
database. In-scope firms must manage cyber and
supply-chain risk and notify authorities of significant
incidents. Management bodies must approve and
oversee cyber risk measures, with potential liability for

infringements.
Cybersecurity Act In force 2019. Establishes an EU-wide certification
framework for ICT products, services, processes and
managed security services, and made the EU
cybersecurity body ENISA permanent.
Cyber Resilience Act In force 2024, main obligations from 2027.
Manufacturers and retailers of products with digital
elements must deliver security by design, a defined
support period and security fixes within it, plus
mandatory vulnerability management and disclosure.

Digital Operational
Resilience Act (DORA)
In force 2023, applies from 2025. Financial-services
specific, covering own-firm and third-party risk. Requires
an ICT business continuity policy, regular resilience
testing and disclosure of major incidents, and gives
regulators oversight of critical ICT providers.
Cyber Solidarity Act In force 2025. Creates a European Cybersecurity Alert
System to detect threats and share cross-border
warnings, plus a Cyber Emergency Mechanism to boost

preparedness and response.
Regulation Key requirements
White House Accord on
Super Intelligence
(2026)
Emphasises self-regulation to ensure AI safety, with a call for
external evaluations and the possibility of formal regulation in
future. Not legally binding; signed by President Trump and
leading AI companies.
Executive Order on
Cybersecurity (2025)
Highlights risk from foreign actors and orders further action
on defending digital infrastructure, securing the most vital

and governance.
CIRCIA (passed 2022,
not yet in effect)
In-scope entities, focused on critical infrastructure, must
report covered incidents to CISA within 72 hours and
ransomware payments within 24 hours of payment.
Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act
(amended 2024)
Financial institutions must explain information-sharing
practices and safeguard customer data, running a security
programme covering risk assessment, service-provider
oversight and incident response.

Federal Information
Security Modernization
Act (2014)
Applies to federal agencies and contractors: system security
plans, continuous monitoring, incident response capability
and mandatory incident reporting.
Federal Acquisition
Regulation (cyber
provisions 2016)
Governs federal procurement, setting baseline cyber
requirements for suppliers so cyber risk is built into
government contracting. Agencies may impose stricter
provisions.

PREVIEW AND ANALYSIS
Complete Montly CPI will take some time to understand
19 December 2025
Key points
• The October monthly CPI was 
flat in the month, broadly as we 
expected but stronger than market 
expectations. For more information see 
“Electrical noise is far from static”.
• The impact on our inflation profile 
was marginal. The December quarter 
CPI remains 0.6%qtr/3.6%yr, while 
the Trimmed Mean edged up from 
0.7%qtr/3.1%yr to 0.8%qtr/3.2%yr.
• Our November monthly CPI estimate 
is 0.4% for the month, leaving the 
annual pace unchanged at 3.8%yr.
• The introduction of the complete 
monthly CPI is a significant enhancement 
to our analysis of price formation in 
the Australian economy. However, 
with many series having only a short 
history, it will take time to fully 
understand some of the finer details.
• For now, we will continue to place 
greater emphasis on the quarterly 
measures of core inflation.

### Page 2

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts 
are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
No surprise in the October CPI
The new complete monthly CPI gained 3.8% in the year to 
October, coming in a touch softer than Westpac’s estimate of 
3.9%yr but a bit stronger than the market estimate of 3.6%yr. 
So while the market and other commentators may have been 
surprised by the result it was broadly within our expectations 
and as such has had just a marginal impact on our inflation 
profile.
The October CPI was flat in the month, a touch softer than 
Westpac’s published near-cast of 0.1% on the back of a larger 
than expected decline in electricity (–10.2%) which was only 
partially offset by a smaller than expected decline in auto fuel 
(–0.9%) and an unexpected bump up in garments (+2.6%).
The Trimmed Mean (TM) lifted 0.3% in the month of October, 
the same monthly increase it has seen for the previous two 
months and down from the 0.5%mth increase in July but 
stronger than the 0.2%mth prints from March to June.
This saw the annual pace of the TM increase to 3.3% from 
3.2% in September. This month the TM excluded the annual 
rise in electricity; alongside other large price rises and falls. 
Due to the short history of many series in the monthly CPI, the 
annual pace of monthly TM inflation can only be calculated 
back to April 2025. Before then the ABS notes that the annual 
pace is calculated from the quarterly CPI data.
For more information on the October monthly CPI see 
“Electrical noise is far from static ”.
Monthly CPI points to some momentum
Following the October CPI we have seen some minor revisions 
to the various components in our December quarter CPI 
estimate with the upward revisions, such as transport via auto 
fuel and motor vehicles, being offset by downward revisions 
to recreation, alcohol & tobacco and utilities in particular. This 
has left our December CPI estimate unchanged at 0.6%qtr.   
As our Chief Economist, Luci Ellis, has noted that inflation 
did see a bump in the September quarter and again in the 
month of October. However, the main sources of the surprise 
have had little to do with domestic demand or labour market 
pressures. Rather, a sizeable part of the bump looks to have 
been from administered prices and noise. Since the release of 
the data, the RBA has recognised this issue but at the post 
RBA Board Decision Media Conference in December, the 
Governor communicated that the Bank is more worried about 
upside risks to inflation than first thought.
We agree that some of this momentum will continue into the 
December quarter as highlighted by our upward revision in our 
We will continue to focus on the quarterly prints
Justin Smirk
Senior Economist
